You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. Um, yeah, I'm just doing what I absolutely have to do to survive right now, and letting the rest slide. Like laundry... I haven't done laundry in two weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. The lead role started right after our team merger, and within the first month or two, I could just tell I wasn't handling work the way I normally do. Mergers are always such a mess, you know? They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. 

The new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. 

But, uh... yeah, the lead role. That's definitely when all this started. I just wasn't handling the stress like I used to.

I haven't noticed any problems there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms are affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, I've totally stopped showing up for basketball. They use this carbon fiber midfoot shank plate now that's supposed to give you this explosive energy return when you jump. It's crazy because carbon fiber used to be just for aerospace stuff, you know? \n\nI remember watching this documentary on how they weave the carbon filaments and bake them in an autoclave under massive pressure. It's a lot like fiberglass, actually. I helped my uncle patch a fiberglass hull on his bass boat a few summers ago. You have to mix the resin and the hardener at the exact right ratio, depending on the ambient temperature, otherwise it just stays tacky forever and ruins the patch. We used this marine-grade epoxy that smelled awful. You definitely need a respirator with organic vapor cartridges when you work with that stuff. \n\nBut anyway, sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, my free time. Yeah, I'm not playing basketball, and my usual sketching time is just gone. Work is just completely taking over my life right now. It's bleeding into everything, and I can't even keep up there, either. My supervisor actually had to pull me aside for a performance check-in because of those missed cycle-count deadlines I was talking about earlier. So, yeah... I'm not really doing anything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, no, not really. I mean, I eat about the same, I guess. I usually just grab something quick, you know? Like, I've been eating a lot of those frozen burritos lately. The El Monterey ones, beef and bean. You have to microwave them for exactly one minute and forty-five seconds, but then the trick is you have to let them sit for at least two minutes so the heat distributes, otherwise the ends are lava and the middle is an ice cube. It's all about thermal conductivity. \n\nActually, I was reading about microwave magnetrons the other day. It's fascinating how they generate electromagnetic waves at 2.45 gigahertz, which is the exact frequency needed to agitate water molecules. It's the same frequency range that Wi-Fi routers use, which is why your internet can sometimes drop if you're streaming a video and someone turns on the microwave. I had to upgrade my router to a dual-band system, one of those mesh networks with the satellite nodes? I put one in the living room and one out in the garage because the signal couldn't penetrate the cinderblock walls. Cinderblock is terrible for RF signals because of the density and the rebar inside it. It acts almost like a Faraday cage. \n\nBut, uh, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, food. Yeah, my appetite hasn't really changed. I just eat when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to fulfill your responsibilities and obligations outside of work, like with friends and family, as well as you were before taking the acting workflow lead role?\n\nPatient: Responsibilities outside of work? Lithium-ion is so much better because it has a higher energy density and no memory effect. I actually replaced the lithium-ion battery in my smartphone myself a few months ago. I mean, my mom asked me to come over and help her clean out the gutters last weekend, and I just... I couldn't do it. I told her I was too tired. They're supposed to be low maintenance, but the problem is the downspouts always get clogged with these specific oak leaves from the tree in her front yard. \n\nOak leaves take forever to decompose because they have high levels of tannins in them. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. Yeah, I didn't go help her. I just stayed on the couch. So, yeah, I guess I'm failing there, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to take care of your daily responsibilities because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Daily responsibilities... I mean, I'm doing the bare minimum, I guess. Like, I'm still paying my bills, mostly because they're on autopay. Autopay is a lifesaver, honestly. I set it up through my credit union a few years ago. Credit unions are so much better than traditional banks because they're not-for-profit, so they usually have better interest rates on auto loans. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. It uses this steel belt and two pulleys that change diameter to keep the engine in its optimal power band. But the problem with CVTs is that if the belt slips or stretches even a little bit, the whole thing is basically toast. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. Um, yeah, I'm just doing what I absolutely have to do to survive right now, and letting the rest slide. Like laundry... I haven't done laundry in two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started as a direct reaction to becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. That's definitely when all this started. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Overwhelmed... yeah, I'd say so. It just feels like there's too much to do and not enough time, you know? Like, the warehouse is constantly getting these huge shipments of pallets, and trying to keep track of it all is a nightmare. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. 

The standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. 

Wait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. Yeah, I'm definitely struggling to keep up with everything right now. It's just a lot.

You have to microwave them for exactly one minute and forty-five seconds, but then the trick is you have to let them sit for at least two minutes so the heat distributes, otherwise the ends are lava and the middle is an ice cube. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. I just wasn't handling the stress like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your responsibilities and struggling to cope with the demands of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed... yeah, I'd say so. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. \n\nThe standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. They're supposed to be low maintenance, but the problem is the downspouts always get clogged with these specific oak leaves from the tree in her front yard. \n\nOak leaves take forever to decompose because they have high levels of tannins in them. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. Yeah, I didn't go help her. I just stayed on the couch. So, yeah, I guess I'm failing there, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to take care of your daily responsibilities because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Daily responsibilities... I mean, I'm doing the bare minimum, I guess. Like, I'm still paying my bills, mostly because they're on autopay. Autopay is a lifesaver, honestly. I set it up through my credit union a few years ago. Credit unions are so much better than traditional banks because they're not-for-profit, so they usually have better interest rates on auto loans. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. It uses this steel belt and two pulleys that change diameter to keep the engine in its optimal power band. But the problem with CVTs is that if the belt slips or stretches even a little bit, the whole thing is basically toast. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. Um, yeah, I'm just doing what I absolutely have to do to survive right now, and letting the rest slide. Like laundry... I haven't done laundry in two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started as a direct reaction to becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The lead role started right after our team merger, and within the first month or two, I could just tell I wasn't handling work the way I normally do. Mergers are always such a mess, you know? They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. That's definitely when all this started. I just wasn't handling the stress like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your responsibilities and struggling to cope with the demands of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed... yeah, I'd say so. It just feels like there's too much to do and not enough time, you know? Like, the warehouse is constantly getting these huge shipments of pallets, and trying to keep track of it all is a nightmare. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. \n\nThe standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. Yeah, I'm definitely struggling to keep up with everything right now. It's just a lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoiding tasks... I mean, I don't know if I'm avoiding specific things, exactly. I just try to keep my head down and get through the shift. But honestly, the whole environment is just... it's a lot. Especially the loading dock area. The noise out there is incredible. We have these massive bay doors that roll up, and the springs on them are so loud. 

They use torsion springs, which are actually really dangerous if you don't know what you're doing. A torsion spring stores mechanical energy by twisting, unlike extension springs that stretch. The amount of torque they hold is insane. If one of those snaps while you're standing near it, it can literally take your head off. That's why garage door repair is one of the most dangerous DIY projects you can do. Most people don't realize that the spring is doing all the heavy lifting, not the motor. The motor just guides the door. The spring acts as a counterbalance to the weight of the door. It's the same principle as a trebuchet, really, using a counterweight to launch a projectile. Trebuchets were the ultimate siege weapons in the Middle Ages. They could hurl a 200-pound stone over 300 yards. They even used to launch diseased animal carcasses over castle walls as an early form of biological warfare, which is pretty dark when you think about it. The physics of a trebuchet is fascinating, though, because it uses a sling at the end of the throwing arm to effectively double the length of the arm right before the release point, which massively increases the velocity of the projectile. 

Sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things at work. Yeah, I don't think there's any one specific thing I'm avoiding. I'm just... I'm just trying to get through the day without losing my mind, you know?

You find these rusty old skillets at yard sales for like five bucks, and you can strip them down using an electrolysis tank. \n\nI built my own tank in the garage using a manual battery charger and some scrap rebar for the anodes. You hook it up, drop the pan in a washing soda solution, and it literally pulls the rust right off the metal. It's fascinating chemistry, really. Then you just season it with some grapeseed oil—grapeseed is way better than flaxseed, trust me, flaxseed flakes off too easily—and bake it at 450 degrees for an hour. They're way better than standard box fans because they use this deep-pitched propeller design that actually moves the air in a tight beam across the room. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. Especially the loading dock area. The noise out there is incredible. We have these massive bay doors that roll up, and the springs on them are so loud. \n\nThey use torsion springs, which are actually really dangerous if you don't know what you're doing. A torsion spring stores mechanical energy by twisting, unlike extension springs that stretch. The amount of torque they hold is insane. If one of those snaps while you're standing near it, it can literally take your head off. That's why garage door repair is one of the most dangerous DIY projects you can do. Most people don't realize that the spring is doing all the heavy lifting, not the motor. The motor just guides the door. The spring acts as a counterbalance to the weight of the door. It's the same principle as a trebuchet, really, using a counterweight to launch a projectile. Trebuchets were the ultimate siege weapons in the Middle Ages. They could hurl a 200-pound stone over 300 yards. They even used to launch diseased animal carcasses over castle walls as an early form of biological warfare, which is pretty dark when you think about it. The physics of a trebuchet is fascinating, though, because it uses a sling at the end of the throwing arm to effectively double the length of the arm right before the release point, which massively increases the velocity of the projectile. \n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Smelled like burnt ozone. It actually reminded me of when my buddy Dave tried to wire up a 220-volt outlet in his garage for a heavy-duty air compressor and got the poles mixed up. Tripped the main breaker for his whole house. He's lucky he didn't start a fire. We ended up having to call a licensed electrician, this guy Mike who used to play in a bowling league with my dad. Great guy, throws a mean hook. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what was the question? Oh, sleeping. Yeah, no, I sleep fine. Like I said, I'm usually just so exhausted from the warehouse that I sleep straight through. I haven't noticed any problems there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms are affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned earlier, I've totally stopped showing up for basketball. The guys keep texting me to see where I am, but I just... I don't go. Actually, my buddy Greg just bought those new LeBron 20s for our pickup games. Man, sneaker technology has gotten insane. They use this carbon fiber midfoot shank plate now that's supposed to give you this explosive energy return when you jump. It's crazy because carbon fiber used to be just for aerospace stuff, you know? \n\nI remember watching this documentary on how they weave the carbon filaments and bake them in an autoclave under massive pressure. It's a lot like fiberglass, actually. I helped my uncle patch a fiberglass hull on his bass boat a few summers ago. You have to mix the resin and the hardener at the exact right ratio, depending on the ambient temperature, otherwise it just stays tacky forever and ruins the patch. We used this marine-grade epoxy that smelled awful. You definitely need a respirator with organic vapor cartridges when you work with that stuff. \n\nBut anyway, sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, my free time. Yeah, I'm not playing basketball, and my usual sketching time is just gone. Work is just completely taking over my life right now. It's bleeding into everything, and I can't even keep up there, either. My supervisor actually had to pull me aside for a performance check-in because of those missed cycle-count deadlines I was talking about earlier. So, yeah... I'm not really doing anything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, no, not really. I mean, I eat about the same, I guess. I usually just grab something quick, you know? Like, I've been eating a lot of those frozen burritos lately. The El Monterey ones, beef and bean. You have to microwave them for exactly one minute and forty-five seconds, but then the trick is you have to let them sit for at least two minutes so the heat distributes, otherwise the ends are lava and the middle is an ice cube. It's all about thermal conductivity. \n\nActually, I was reading about microwave magnetrons the other day. It's fascinating how they generate electromagnetic waves at 2.45 gigahertz, which is the exact frequency needed to agitate water molecules. It's the same frequency range that Wi-Fi routers use, which is why your internet can sometimes drop if you're streaming a video and someone turns on the microwave. I had to upgrade my router to a dual-band system, one of those mesh networks with the satellite nodes? I put one in the living room and one out in the garage because the signal couldn't penetrate the cinderblock walls. Cinderblock is terrible for RF signals because of the density and the rebar inside it. It acts almost like a Faraday cage. \n\nBut, uh, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, food. Yeah, my appetite hasn't really changed. I just eat when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to fulfill your responsibilities and obligations outside of work, like with friends and family, as well as you were before taking the acting workflow lead role?\n\nPatient: Responsibilities outside of work? Man, I don't even know anymore. Like I said earlier, work is just completely taking over my life. I've stopped showing up for basketball entirely, and my usual sketching time is just gone. And it's not even like I'm doing a good job at work to justify it, you know? Like I mentioned, my supervisor actually had to pull me aside for a performance check-in because of those missed cycle-count deadlines. \n\nActually, speaking of cycle counts, we use these handheld RF scanners in the warehouse to track inventory, and the batteries on those things are absolute garbage. They use these old nickel-cadmium packs that develop a \"memory\" if you don't discharge them all the way before putting them on the charger. It's such an outdated technology. Lithium-ion is so much better because it has a higher energy density and no memory effect. I actually replaced the lithium-ion battery in my smartphone myself a few months ago. You have to use a heat gun to soften the adhesive around the screen, and then you use these little plastic guitar picks to pry it open without cracking the glass. It's super delicate work. I accidentally nicked the ribbon cable for the fingerprint sensor on my first try and had to order a replacement part from this supplier in Shenzhen. Took three weeks to arrive, but the tracking updates were fascinating. It went through like four different sorting facilities before it even got on a cargo plane. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, responsibilities. Yeah, no, I'm definitely not handling things well outside of work. Or at work, really. It's just all bleeding together and I'm dropping the ball everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing to meet your personal or family obligations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Personal or family obligations... yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom asked me to come over and help her clean out the gutters last weekend, and I just... I couldn't do it. I told her I was too tired. Which is true, but I also just didn't want to deal with it. Gutters are such a pain, especially the seamless aluminum ones she has. They're supposed to be low maintenance, but the problem is the downspouts always get clogged with these specific oak leaves from the tree in her front yard. \n\nOak leaves take forever to decompose because they have high levels of tannins in them. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. Yeah, I didn't go help her. I just stayed on the couch. So, yeah, I guess I'm failing there, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to take care of your daily responsibilities because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Daily responsibilities... I mean, I'm doing the bare minimum, I guess. Like, I'm still paying my bills, mostly because they're on autopay. Autopay is a lifesaver, honestly. I set it up through my credit union a few years ago. Credit unions are so much better than traditional banks because they're not-for-profit, so they usually have better interest rates on auto loans. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. It uses this steel belt and two pulleys that change diameter to keep the engine in its optimal power band. But the problem with CVTs is that if the belt slips or stretches even a little bit, the whole thing is basically toast. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. Um, yeah, I'm just doing what I absolutely have to do to survive right now, and letting the rest slide. Like laundry... I haven't done laundry in two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started as a direct reaction to becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The lead role started right after our team merger, and within the first month or two, I could just tell I wasn't handling work the way I normally do. Mergers are always such a mess, you know? They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. That's definitely when all this started. I just wasn't handling the stress like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your responsibilities and struggling to cope with the demands of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed... yeah, I'd say so. It just feels like there's too much to do and not enough time, you know? Like, the warehouse is constantly getting these huge shipments of pallets, and trying to keep track of it all is a nightmare. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. \n\nThe standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. Yeah, I'm definitely struggling to keep up with everything right now. It's just a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any specific tasks or situations at work because they make you feel uncomfortable or stressed?\n\nPatient: Avoiding tasks... I mean, I don't know if I'm avoiding specific things, exactly. I just try to keep my head down and get through the shift. But honestly, the whole environment is just... it's a lot. Especially the loading dock area. The noise out there is incredible. We have these massive bay doors that roll up, and the springs on them are so loud. \n\nThey use torsion springs, which are actually really dangerous if you don't know what you're doing. A torsion spring stores mechanical energy by twisting, unlike extension springs that stretch. The amount of torque they hold is insane. If one of those snaps while you're standing near it, it can literally take your head off. That's why garage door repair is one of the most dangerous DIY projects you can do. Most people don't realize that the spring is doing all the heavy lifting, not the motor. The motor just guides the door. The spring acts as a counterbalance to the weight of the door. It's the same principle as a trebuchet, really, using a counterweight to launch a projectile. Trebuchets were the ultimate siege weapons in the Middle Ages. They could hurl a 200-pound stone over 300 yards. They even used to launch diseased animal carcasses over castle walls as an early form of biological warfare, which is pretty dark when you think about it. The physics of a trebuchet is fascinating, though, because it uses a sling at the end of the throwing arm to effectively double the length of the arm right before the release point, which massively increases the velocity of the projectile. \n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things at work. Yeah, I don't think there's any one specific thing I'm avoiding. I'm just... I'm just trying to get through the day without losing my mind, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Improve? Yeah, probably. I mean, the acting lead responsibilities are still totally in place right now, so it's not like the work change ended and I'm still feeling this way after the fact, you know? I'm still dealing with the change every single day. 

It's funny they call it "acting" lead, though. It’s just a temporary label they slap on you so they don't have to bump your pay grade. Speaking of labels, the new thermal shipping labels they make us use in the warehouse have this insanely cheap adhesive. It’s supposed to be temporary, but it leaves this gross, sticky residue on the scanners. Adhesives are actually really complex chemistry. There are basically two main types of pressure-sensitive adhesives: rubber-based and acrylic. Rubber-based has a really high initial "tack," meaning it sticks fast, but it completely breaks down under UV light. That’s why old masking tape turns into that crusty yellow dust if you leave it on a window. 

Acrylic adhesives take longer to cure, but they can withstand extreme temperatures and weather. They actually use structural acrylic tape instead of rivets or welding on some airplane panels and skyscrapers now. It’s wild to think they hold up the exterior glass on the Burj Khalifa with basically industrial double-sided tape. That building is so tall that you can watch the sunset on the ground floor, take the elevator up, and watch the exact same sunset all over again at the top. The elevators go like 22 miles per hour, which causes massive changes in air pressure, so they had to design special aerodynamics for the shafts just so people's eardrums wouldn't pop... 

Wait, sorry, what did you ask me? If I'd feel better? Right. Yeah, like I said, since the acting role hasn't ended, I'm still in the thick of it every day. So, I guess if they finally took it off my plate and I wasn't doing it anymore, things would probably get a lot better.

You have to microwave them for exactly one minute and forty-five seconds, but then the trick is you have to let them sit for at least two minutes so the heat distributes, otherwise the ends are lava and the middle is an ice cube. It's all about thermal conductivity. \n\nActually, I was reading about microwave magnetrons the other day. It's fascinating how they generate electromagnetic waves at 2.45 gigahertz, which is the exact frequency needed to agitate water molecules. Yeah, my appetite hasn't really changed. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. Yeah, I didn't go help her. I just stayed on the couch. I mean, I'm doing the bare minimum, I guess. Like, I'm still paying my bills, mostly because they're on autopay. Autopay is a lifesaver, honestly. I set it up through my credit union a few years ago. Credit unions are so much better than traditional banks because they're not-for-profit, so they usually have better interest rates on auto loans. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. The lead role started right after our team merger, and within the first month or two, I could just tell I wasn't handling work the way I normally do. Mergers are always such a mess, you know? They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. That's definitely when all this started. I just wasn't handling the stress like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your responsibilities and struggling to cope with the demands of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed... yeah, I'd say so. It just feels like there's too much to do and not enough time, you know? Like, the warehouse is constantly getting these huge shipments of pallets, and trying to keep track of it all is a nightmare. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. \n\nThe standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. Yeah, I'm definitely struggling to keep up with everything right now. It's just a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any specific tasks or situations at work because they make you feel uncomfortable or stressed?\n\nPatient: Avoiding tasks... I mean, I don't know if I'm avoiding specific things, exactly. I just try to keep my head down and get through the shift. But honestly, the whole environment is just... it's a lot. Especially the loading dock area. The noise out there is incredible. We have these massive bay doors that roll up, and the springs on them are so loud. \n\nThey use torsion springs, which are actually really dangerous if you don't know what you're doing. A torsion spring stores mechanical energy by twisting, unlike extension springs that stretch. The amount of torque they hold is insane. If one of those snaps while you're standing near it, it can literally take your head off. That's why garage door repair is one of the most dangerous DIY projects you can do. Most people don't realize that the spring is doing all the heavy lifting, not the motor. The motor just guides the door. The spring acts as a counterbalance to the weight of the door. It's the same principle as a trebuchet, really, using a counterweight to launch a projectile. Trebuchets were the ultimate siege weapons in the Middle Ages. They could hurl a 200-pound stone over 300 yards. They even used to launch diseased animal carcasses over castle walls as an early form of biological warfare, which is pretty dark when you think about it. The physics of a trebuchet is fascinating, though, because it uses a sling at the end of the throwing arm to effectively double the length of the arm right before the release point, which massively increases the velocity of the projectile. \n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things at work. Yeah, I don't think there's any one specific thing I'm avoiding. I'm just... I'm just trying to get through the day without losing my mind, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if your work situation changed, for example, if you were no longer the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Improve? Yeah, probably. I mean, the acting lead responsibilities are still totally in place right now, so it's not like the work change ended and I'm still feeling this way after the fact, you know? I'm still dealing with the change every single day. \n\nIt's funny they call it \"acting\" lead, though. It’s just a temporary label they slap on you so they don't have to bump your pay grade. Speaking of labels, the new thermal shipping labels they make us use in the warehouse have this insanely cheap adhesive. It’s supposed to be temporary, but it leaves this gross, sticky residue on the scanners. Adhesives are actually really complex chemistry. There are basically two main types of pressure-sensitive adhesives: rubber-based and acrylic. Rubber-based has a really high initial \"tack,\" meaning it sticks fast, but it completely breaks down under UV light. That’s why old masking tape turns into that crusty yellow dust if you leave it on a window. \n\nAcrylic adhesives take longer to cure, but they can withstand extreme temperatures and weather. They actually use structural acrylic tape instead of rivets or welding on some airplane panels and skyscrapers now. It’s wild to think they hold up the exterior glass on the Burj Khalifa with basically industrial double-sided tape. That building is so tall that you can watch the sunset on the ground floor, take the elevator up, and watch the exact same sunset all over again at the top. The elevators go like 22 miles per hour, which causes massive changes in air pressure, so they had to design special aerodynamics for the shafts just so people's eardrums wouldn't pop... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? If I'd feel better? Right. Yeah, like I said, since the acting role hasn't ended, I'm still in the thick of it every day. So, I guess if they finally took it off my plate and I wasn't doing it anymore, things would probably get a lot better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... I really think they would. If I could just go back to my old position, I'd feel like myself again. But like I was saying before, the acting lead responsibilities are still totally in place right now. It hasn't ended. I'm still dealing with this change every single day, so I haven't had a chance to see if I'd feel better after the fact, you know? 

It's just constant pressure. Which reminds me of how they measure pressure in the hydraulic lifts we use for the heavy pallets. They use PSI, pounds per square inch, and those hydraulic fluid lines are under incredible stress. Hydraulics are fascinating because liquids are practically incompressible. That's Pascal's principle. If you apply pressure to a confined fluid, the pressure change occurs everywhere within the fluid. That's how a small piston can lift a massive amount of weight on a larger piston. It's the same concept behind the brakes in your car. When you step on the brake pedal, you're pushing a small piston into the master cylinder, which forces brake fluid through the lines down to the calipers. The calipers have larger pistons that squeeze the brake pads against the rotors. 

Brake fluid is actually super corrosive, though. It's usually a glycol-ether based fluid, and if you spill it on your car's paint, it will eat right through the clear coat in a matter of minutes. It's also hygroscopic, which means it absorbs moisture from the air. That's why you're supposed to flush your brake fluid every couple of years. If it absorbs too much water, the boiling point of the fluid drops. Then, if you're braking hard down a steep hill, the heat from the rotors can actually boil the water in the lines, creating steam. And unlike liquid, steam is a gas, which *is* compressible. So if that happens, your brake pedal just goes straight to the floor and you have no brakes at all. It's called brake fade, and it's terrifying. 

Oh, sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Returning to my old job? Yeah, if things went back to normal, I really think all this stress would just go away. But right now, I'm still stuck in it every day.

Like I mentioned, my supervisor actually had to pull me aside for a performance check-in because of those missed cycle-count deadlines. \n\nActually, speaking of cycle counts, we use these handheld RF scanners in the warehouse to track inventory, and the batteries on those things are absolute garbage. They use these old nickel-cadmium packs that develop a \"memory\" if you don't discharge them all the way before putting them on the charger. It's such an outdated technology. Lithium-ion is so much better because it has a higher energy density and no memory effect. I actually replaced the lithium-ion battery in my smartphone myself a few months ago. You have to use a heat gun to soften the adhesive around the screen, and then you use these little plastic guitar picks to pry it open without cracking the glass. It's super delicate work. I accidentally nicked the ribbon cable for the fingerprint sensor on my first try and had to order a replacement part from this supplier in Shenzhen. Took three weeks to arrive, but the tracking updates were fascinating. It went through like four different sorting facilities before it even got on a cargo plane. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, responsibilities. Yeah, no, I'm definitely not handling things well outside of work. Or at work, really. It's just all bleeding together and I'm dropping the ball everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing to meet your personal or family obligations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Personal or family obligations... yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom asked me to come over and help her clean out the gutters last weekend, and I just... I couldn't do it. I told her I was too tired. Which is true, but I also just didn't want to deal with it. Gutters are such a pain, especially the seamless aluminum ones she has. They're supposed to be low maintenance, but the problem is the downspouts always get clogged with these specific oak leaves from the tree in her front yard. \n\nOak leaves take forever to decompose because they have high levels of tannins in them. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. Yeah, I didn't go help her. I just stayed on the couch. So, yeah, I guess I'm failing there, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to take care of your daily responsibilities because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Daily responsibilities... I mean, I'm doing the bare minimum, I guess. Like, I'm still paying my bills, mostly because they're on autopay. Autopay is a lifesaver, honestly. I set it up through my credit union a few years ago. Credit unions are so much better than traditional banks because they're not-for-profit, so they usually have better interest rates on auto loans. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. It uses this steel belt and two pulleys that change diameter to keep the engine in its optimal power band. But the problem with CVTs is that if the belt slips or stretches even a little bit, the whole thing is basically toast. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. Um, yeah, I'm just doing what I absolutely have to do to survive right now, and letting the rest slide. Like laundry... I haven't done laundry in two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started as a direct reaction to becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The lead role started right after our team merger, and within the first month or two, I could just tell I wasn't handling work the way I normally do. Mergers are always such a mess, you know? They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. \n\nThe standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. I mean, I don't know if I'm avoiding specific things, exactly. I just try to keep my head down and get through the shift. But honestly, the whole environment is just... it's a lot. Especially the loading dock area. The noise out there is incredible. We have these massive bay doors that roll up, and the springs on them are so loud. \n\nThey use torsion springs, which are actually really dangerous if you don't know what you're doing. A torsion spring stores mechanical energy by twisting, unlike extension springs that stretch. The amount of torque they hold is insane. If one of those snaps while you're standing near it, it can literally take your head off. That's why garage door repair is one of the most dangerous DIY projects you can do. Most people don't realize that the spring is doing all the heavy lifting, not the motor. The motor just guides the door. The spring acts as a counterbalance to the weight of the door. It's the same principle as a trebuchet, really, using a counterweight to launch a projectile. Trebuchets were the ultimate siege weapons in the Middle Ages. They could hurl a 200-pound stone over 300 yards. They even used to launch diseased animal carcasses over castle walls as an early form of biological warfare, which is pretty dark when you think about it. The physics of a trebuchet is fascinating, though, because it uses a sling at the end of the throwing arm to effectively double the length of the arm right before the release point, which massively increases the velocity of the projectile. \n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things at work. Yeah, I don't think there's any one specific thing I'm avoiding. I'm just... I'm just trying to get through the day without losing my mind, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if your work situation changed, for example, if you were no longer the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Improve? I'm still dealing with the change every single day. \n\nIt's funny they call it \"acting\" lead, though. It’s just a temporary label they slap on you so they don't have to bump your pay grade. Speaking of labels, the new thermal shipping labels they make us use in the warehouse have this insanely cheap adhesive. It’s supposed to be temporary, but it leaves this gross, sticky residue on the scanners. Adhesives are actually really complex chemistry. There are basically two main types of pressure-sensitive adhesives: rubber-based and acrylic. Rubber-based has a really high initial \"tack,\" meaning it sticks fast, but it completely breaks down under UV light. That’s why old masking tape turns into that crusty yellow dust if you leave it on a window. \n\nAcrylic adhesives take longer to cure, but they can withstand extreme temperatures and weather. They actually use structural acrylic tape instead of rivets or welding on some airplane panels and skyscrapers now. It’s wild to think they hold up the exterior glass on the Burj Khalifa with basically industrial double-sided tape. That building is so tall that you can watch the sunset on the ground floor, take the elevator up, and watch the exact same sunset all over again at the top. The elevators go like 22 miles per hour, which causes massive changes in air pressure, so they had to design special aerodynamics for the shafts just so people's eardrums wouldn't pop... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? If I'd feel better? Right. Yeah, like I said, since the acting role hasn't ended, I'm still in the thick of it every day. So, I guess if they finally took it off my plate and I wasn't doing it anymore, things would probably get a lot better.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would go away if your work situation returned to what it was before you became the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I really think they would. If I could just go back to my old position, I'd feel like myself again. But like I was saying before, the acting lead responsibilities are still totally in place right now. It hasn't ended. I'm still dealing with this change every single day, so I haven't had a chance to see if I'd feel better after the fact, you know? \n\nIt's just constant pressure. Which reminds me of how they measure pressure in the hydraulic lifts we use for the heavy pallets. They use PSI, pounds per square inch, and those hydraulic fluid lines are under incredible stress. Hydraulics are fascinating because liquids are practically incompressible. That's Pascal's principle. If you apply pressure to a confined fluid, the pressure change occurs everywhere within the fluid. That's how a small piston can lift a massive amount of weight on a larger piston. It's the same concept behind the brakes in your car. When you step on the brake pedal, you're pushing a small piston into the master cylinder, which forces brake fluid through the lines down to the calipers. The calipers have larger pistons that squeeze the brake pads against the rotors. \n\nBrake fluid is actually super corrosive, though. It's usually a glycol-ether based fluid, and if you spill it on your car's paint, it will eat right through the clear coat in a matter of minutes. It's also hygroscopic, which means it absorbs moisture from the air. That's why you're supposed to flush your brake fluid every couple of years. If it absorbs too much water, the boiling point of the fluid drops. Then, if you're braking hard down a steep hill, the heat from the rotors can actually boil the water in the lines, creating steam. And unlike liquid, steam is a gas, which *is* compressible. So if that happens, your brake pedal just goes straight to the floor and you have no brakes at all. It's called brake fade, and it's terrifying. \n\nOh, sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Returning to my old job? Yeah, if things went back to normal, I really think all this stress would just go away. But right now, I'm still stuck in it every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's the same frequency range that Wi-Fi routers use, which is why your internet can sometimes drop if you're streaming a video and someone turns on the microwave. I had to upgrade my router to a dual-band system, one of those mesh networks with the satellite nodes? I put one in the living room and one out in the garage because the signal couldn't penetrate the cinderblock walls. Cinderblock is terrible for RF signals because of the density and the rebar inside it. It acts almost like a Faraday cage. \n\nBut, uh, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, food. Yeah, my appetite hasn't really changed. I just eat when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to fulfill your responsibilities and obligations outside of work, like with friends and family, as well as you were before taking the acting workflow lead role?\n\nPatient: Responsibilities outside of work? Man, I don't even know anymore. Like I said earlier, work is just completely taking over my life. I've stopped showing up for basketball entirely, and my usual sketching time is just gone. And it's not even like I'm doing a good job at work to justify it, you know? Like I mentioned, my supervisor actually had to pull me aside for a performance check-in because of those missed cycle-count deadlines. \n\nActually, speaking of cycle counts, we use these handheld RF scanners in the warehouse to track inventory, and the batteries on those things are absolute garbage. They use these old nickel-cadmium packs that develop a \"memory\" if you don't discharge them all the way before putting them on the charger. It's such an outdated technology. Lithium-ion is so much better because it has a higher energy density and no memory effect. I actually replaced the lithium-ion battery in my smartphone myself a few months ago. You have to use a heat gun to soften the adhesive around the screen, and then you use these little plastic guitar picks to pry it open without cracking the glass. It's super delicate work. I accidentally nicked the ribbon cable for the fingerprint sensor on my first try and had to order a replacement part from this supplier in Shenzhen. Took three weeks to arrive, but the tracking updates were fascinating. It went through like four different sorting facilities before it even got on a cargo plane. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, responsibilities. Yeah, no, I'm definitely not handling things well outside of work. Or at work, really. It's just all bleeding together and I'm dropping the ball everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing to meet your personal or family obligations because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Personal or family obligations... yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom asked me to come over and help her clean out the gutters last weekend, and I just... I couldn't do it. I told her I was too tired. Which is true, but I also just didn't want to deal with it. Gutters are such a pain, especially the seamless aluminum ones she has. They're supposed to be low maintenance, but the problem is the downspouts always get clogged with these specific oak leaves from the tree in her front yard. \n\nOak leaves take forever to decompose because they have high levels of tannins in them. It's the same stuff they use to tan leather, actually. I went to a living history museum once and saw how they used to soak animal hides in these vats of oak bark tea for months. The smell was unbelievable. It's a chemical process where the tannins bind to the collagen proteins in the hide, which stops it from rotting and makes it flexible. It's crazy to think people figured that out thousands of years ago just by trial and error. Kind of like how they figured out smelting iron from bog ore. You have to get the temperature up to like 2,800 degrees Fahrenheit, which requires a constant supply of air from a bellows. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, my mom's gutters. Yeah, I didn't go help her. I just stayed on the couch. So, yeah, I guess I'm failing there, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to take care of your daily responsibilities because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Daily responsibilities... I mean, I'm doing the bare minimum, I guess. Like, I'm still paying my bills, mostly because they're on autopay. Autopay is a lifesaver, honestly. I set it up through my credit union a few years ago. Credit unions are so much better than traditional banks because they're not-for-profit, so they usually have better interest rates on auto loans. I had to get a new car loan last year when my old Honda finally died. The transmission just gave out completely. \n\nIt's a continuously variable transmission, a CVT, which is supposed to be more fuel-efficient because it doesn't have fixed gears. It uses this steel belt and two pulleys that change diameter to keep the engine in its optimal power band. But the problem with CVTs is that if the belt slips or stretches even a little bit, the whole thing is basically toast. You can't really rebuild them easily, you just have to replace the entire unit, which costs like three grand. I actually spent a whole weekend watching teardown videos of CVTs on YouTube. The engineering is pretty brilliant, honestly, how they use hydraulic pressure to adjust the pulley sheaves. It's kind of like how a drill press changes speeds, but infinitely variable.\n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Daily responsibilities? Right. Um, yeah, I'm just doing what I absolutely have to do to survive right now, and letting the rest slide. Like laundry... I haven't done laundry in two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started as a direct reaction to becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. The lead role started right after our team merger, and within the first month or two, I could just tell I wasn't handling work the way I normally do. Mergers are always such a mess, you know? They brought in this whole new warehouse management software system to try and unify everything, and it was a total disaster at first. \n\nThe new software is cloud-based, which sounds great until the Wi-Fi in the warehouse goes down, which it does constantly because of all the metal racking. Metal acts like a Faraday cage and blocks the radio frequency signals. A Faraday cage is basically an enclosure made of conductive material, named after Michael Faraday, the scientist who invented it in like the 1830s. He's the same guy who discovered electromagnetic induction, which is the principle behind electric motors and transformers. Without him, we wouldn't have the modern power grid. The power grid is actually incredibly fragile. It operates on a delicate balance of supply and demand, and if there's too much load on the system, the frequency drops below 60 hertz, and these massive relays automatically trip to prevent the generators from tearing themselves apart. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, the lead role. That's definitely when all this started. I just wasn't handling the stress like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed by your responsibilities and struggling to cope with the demands of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed... yeah, I'd say so. It just feels like there's too much to do and not enough time, you know? Like, the warehouse is constantly getting these huge shipments of pallets, and trying to keep track of it all is a nightmare. Especially when they use those cheap wooden pallets that break if you look at them wrong. \n\nThe standard GMA pallet is supposed to hold up to 4,600 pounds, but the ones we get are usually made of this soft pine that splinters everywhere. Pine is a softwood, which means it comes from gymnosperm trees, like conifers. Hardwoods come from angiosperms, which are trees that produce seeds with some sort of covering, like an acorn or an apple. Oak is a hardwood, obviously. It's crazy how dense oak is compared to pine. That's why they use oak for aging whiskey and wine, because it's watertight but still allows a tiny bit of oxygen to permeate the wood, which mellows out the harsh tannins in the alcohol over time. The whole barrel-making process is actually called cooperage, and it's a dying art. A master cooper has to shape the staves perfectly over an open fire without burning them, just using water and heat to bend the wood. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Overwhelmed? Oh, right. Yeah, I'm definitely struggling to keep up with everything right now. It's just a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any specific tasks or situations at work because they make you feel uncomfortable or stressed?\n\nPatient: Avoiding tasks... I mean, I don't know if I'm avoiding specific things, exactly. I just try to keep my head down and get through the shift. But honestly, the whole environment is just... it's a lot. Especially the loading dock area. The noise out there is incredible. We have these massive bay doors that roll up, and the springs on them are so loud. \n\nThey use torsion springs, which are actually really dangerous if you don't know what you're doing. A torsion spring stores mechanical energy by twisting, unlike extension springs that stretch. The amount of torque they hold is insane. If one of those snaps while you're standing near it, it can literally take your head off. That's why garage door repair is one of the most dangerous DIY projects you can do. Most people don't realize that the spring is doing all the heavy lifting, not the motor. The motor just guides the door. The spring acts as a counterbalance to the weight of the door. It's the same principle as a trebuchet, really, using a counterweight to launch a projectile. Trebuchets were the ultimate siege weapons in the Middle Ages. They could hurl a 200-pound stone over 300 yards. They even used to launch diseased animal carcasses over castle walls as an early form of biological warfare, which is pretty dark when you think about it. The physics of a trebuchet is fascinating, though, because it uses a sling at the end of the throwing arm to effectively double the length of the arm right before the release point, which massively increases the velocity of the projectile. \n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Oh, avoiding things at work. Yeah, I don't think there's any one specific thing I'm avoiding. I'm just... I'm just trying to get through the day without losing my mind, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if your work situation changed, for example, if you were no longer the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Improve? Yeah, probably. I mean, the acting lead responsibilities are still totally in place right now, so it's not like the work change ended and I'm still feeling this way after the fact, you know? I'm still dealing with the change every single day. \n\nIt's funny they call it \"acting\" lead, though. It’s just a temporary label they slap on you so they don't have to bump your pay grade. Speaking of labels, the new thermal shipping labels they make us use in the warehouse have this insanely cheap adhesive. It’s supposed to be temporary, but it leaves this gross, sticky residue on the scanners. Adhesives are actually really complex chemistry. There are basically two main types of pressure-sensitive adhesives: rubber-based and acrylic. Rubber-based has a really high initial \"tack,\" meaning it sticks fast, but it completely breaks down under UV light. That’s why old masking tape turns into that crusty yellow dust if you leave it on a window. \n\nAcrylic adhesives take longer to cure, but they can withstand extreme temperatures and weather. They actually use structural acrylic tape instead of rivets or welding on some airplane panels and skyscrapers now. It’s wild to think they hold up the exterior glass on the Burj Khalifa with basically industrial double-sided tape. That building is so tall that you can watch the sunset on the ground floor, take the elevator up, and watch the exact same sunset all over again at the top. The elevators go like 22 miles per hour, which causes massive changes in air pressure, so they had to design special aerodynamics for the shafts just so people's eardrums wouldn't pop... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? If I'd feel better? Right. Yeah, like I said, since the acting role hasn't ended, I'm still in the thick of it every day. So, I guess if they finally took it off my plate and I wasn't doing it anymore, things would probably get a lot better.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would go away if your work situation returned to what it was before you became the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I really think they would. If I could just go back to my old position, I'd feel like myself again. But like I was saying before, the acting lead responsibilities are still totally in place right now. It hasn't ended. I'm still dealing with this change every single day, so I haven't had a chance to see if I'd feel better after the fact, you know? \n\nIt's just constant pressure. Which reminds me of how they measure pressure in the hydraulic lifts we use for the heavy pallets. They use PSI, pounds per square inch, and those hydraulic fluid lines are under incredible stress. Hydraulics are fascinating because liquids are practically incompressible. That's Pascal's principle. If you apply pressure to a confined fluid, the pressure change occurs everywhere within the fluid. That's how a small piston can lift a massive amount of weight on a larger piston. It's the same concept behind the brakes in your car. When you step on the brake pedal, you're pushing a small piston into the master cylinder, which forces brake fluid through the lines down to the calipers. The calipers have larger pistons that squeeze the brake pads against the rotors. \n\nBrake fluid is actually super corrosive, though. It's usually a glycol-ether based fluid, and if you spill it on your car's paint, it will eat right through the clear coat in a matter of minutes. It's also hygroscopic, which means it absorbs moisture from the air. That's why you're supposed to flush your brake fluid every couple of years. If it absorbs too much water, the boiling point of the fluid drops. Then, if you're braking hard down a steep hill, the heat from the rotors can actually boil the water in the lines, creating steam. And unlike liquid, steam is a gas, which *is* compressible. So if that happens, your brake pedal just goes straight to the floor and you have no brakes at all. It's called brake fade, and it's terrifying. \n\nOh, sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Returning to my old job? Yeah, if things went back to normal, I really think all this stress would just go away. But right now, I'm still stuck in it every day."
